p39 Figure 8a displays the typical morphology—before the immersion test, without any microgaps around the inclusions—of one of the inclusions of interest. As indicated in Figure 8b, a slight dissolution of the matrix occurred around the inclusions after a 5 s immersion. Meanwhile, an anisotropy was observed in the matrix dissolution rate around the inclusions. In this study, the sulfide was identified as CaS. Using SEM-EDS analysis in conjunction with the immersion test, it was shown that the matrix near the CaS at the inclusion surface exhibited a relatively larger corrosion rate than that near the rare earth complex oxides (such as LaAlO3) at the initial stage of the pitting corrosion, i.e., r1 < r2. The matrix around the inclusions further dissolved with the ongoing immersion test (Figure 8b,c) and formed ring-shaped pits due to the weakened anisotropy. Therefore, since the sulfides in the inclusions are susceptible to hydrolysis and produce acidic solutions, which accelerates the pitting occurrence, the sulfide attached to the oxide surface is easily transformed into the starting point of the pitting corrosion [9,14].
p41 Figure 9 displays the corrosion morphology of the composite inclusions/matrix after 1 h and 2 h of the immersion test. After the immersion test for 1 h, obvious local corrosion phenomenon occurred around the inclusion, forming a local characteristic region composed of a residual inclusion, dissolved cavity and near-circular corrosion area (Figure 9a). The enlarged area of inclusion revealed minor amounts of flocculent deposits around the inclusion, which indicated that serious localized corrosion occurred around the inclusion (Figure 9b). Combined with SEM-EDS analysis, only a small amount of sulfur element was detected on the inclusions; the residual elements may form inclusions such as (Mg–Al)–Ox, (RE–Zr–Al–Ca)–Ox and TiN. The corrosion products on the sample surface increased significantly after 2 h of immersion testing. According to the SEM results, not all the inclusions were completely covered by the corrosion products. This is attributed to the varying particle sizes of the inclusions. Figure 9c displays the representative morphology of the inclusions after de-rusting. The irregular shape of the residual inclusions and the steeper holes around the inclusions may be related to the faster expansion rate of Cl- in a vertical direction. Furthermore, the morphology of the pits exhibits behavior ranging from near-circular to anisotropic (after rust removal). EDS did not detect any sulfur elements, indicating that all sulfides were completely dissolved, while compounds such as (Mg–Al)–Ox, (RE–Zr–Al–Ca)–Ox and TiN remained undissolved.
p43 Furthermore, after immersion for 1 h, a small amount of corrosion products were found on the surface of the M/A island distributed around the inclusion in the near-circular corrosion area, implying that the corrosion attack occurred in this region (Figure 9a). The micropores were observed around the inclusion after 2 h of immersion (Figure 9d). The microcouples were found to be formed between the M/A constituents and the ferrite matrix, with the ferrite matrix acting as the anode phase and the M/A constituents as the cathode phase [15,46]. The M/A island gradually fell off with the corrosion dissolution of the ferrite matrix.
p44 The elastic mechanical properties of the inclusions involved in this paper are shown in Table 6 and were determined using the first-principles calculation method. According to calculation results, LaAlO3 and La2O7Zr2 have lower bulk modulus than Al2O3 (249.54) and ZrO2 (271.06), and the bulk modulus of LaAlO3 (192.61) is closer to that of the iron matrix (194.76). The results indicate that LaAlO3 has excellent mechanical compatibility with the matrix. Numerous studies have demonstrated that the visible microcrevices and high-density dislocations between Al2O3 and the matrix are due to the significantly higher hardness and irregular shape of Al2O3 compared with the iron matrix [29,67]. Inclusions, such as CaO (114.11), MgO (165.84), TiN (175.02) and CaS (57.05), exhibit lower bulk modulus than the matrix. Rare earth elements can form rare earth oxides and composite inclusions, such as LaAlO3 and La2O7Zr2, due to their stronger affinity for oxygen than Zr, Ti and other deoxidizers [31]. The spheroidization and softening effects of rare earth elements on the inclusions can reduce the stress concentration around the inclusions [32,67,68]. This is consistent with the absence of microcracking that was observed between the inclusions and the matrix (Figure 4 and Figure 8a).
materials-16-00876-t006 Table 6Calculated mechanical properties of the inclusions and Fe matrix.InclusionsBulk Modulus (GPa)Shear Modulus (GPa)Young’s Modulus (GPa)Poisson’s RatioFe (BCC)194.7681.52214.610.32Al2O3249.54152.76380.610.25ZrO2271.06109.14288.680.32La2O7Zr2165.8360.56161.950.34LaAlO3192.61121.10300.350.24CaO114.1178.81192.190.22MgO165.83127.64304.730.19CaS57.0539.0795.420.22TiN175.0279.89208.010.30
p46 In the present work, density of states of the inclusions of interest were calculated to determine the electrical conductivity. It can be seen that the Fe matrix and TiN (red star) have metallic nature, whereas Al2O3, ZrO2, LaAlO3, La2O7Zr2, MgO, CaO, CaS and Al2MgO4 (blue star) exhibit the properties of insulators (Figure 10). The matrix and TiN exhibit electrical conductivity, whereas the other oxidation or sulfide inclusions exhibit the properties of insulators or semiconductors. TiN holds intact in the immersion test, which could be attributed to its insolubility in strong acids at room temperature and pressure [69,70]. In summary, the composite inclusions in the test steel will not form a microcouple effect with the matrix.
p48 Figure 11 presents the work functions of inclusions and the matrix involved in this paper. The work function is defined as the energy difference between the Fermi level and the vacuum level (Figure 11a) of a slab model and represents the minimum energy required to remove an electron from the sample surface [32,67]. Since Fe (110) surface has the lowest surface energy with the largest possible exposed face, the work function of the Fe (110) surface is utilized as the baseline in this paper [71]. The work functions of the low-index crystal surfaces of Al2O3, CaO, MgO, TiN and CaS are almost relatively lower, while the work functions of the low-index crystal surfaces of LaAlO3, La2O7Zr2, ZrO2 and Al2MgO4 are significantly higher than the work function of the Fe (110) surface (Figure 11b). The work function of the typical inclusion combined with the first-principles calculation results shows that the composite inclusion containing rare earth elements has a higher work function, which implies that it is less likely to act as an anode phase compared to CaS. Furthermore, the work function value corresponds to the phenomena observed in the immersion test, in which CaS was preferentially dissolved and (Mg–Al)–Ox and (RE–Zr–Al–Ca)–Ox remained undissolved (Figure 9d). The addition of rare earth elements can improve inclusion corrosion resistance [18].
p50 Figure 12I illustrates the evolution process of the inclusions involved in this paper. The addition of Zr–Fe, Ti–Fe, Ca–Fe, and RE alloys during the smelting process modified the Al2O3 inclusions into composite inclusions. CaS and TiN precipitated on the previously formed inclusions at a later stage of solidification, ultimately forming composite inclusions with a core–shell structure [12,14,72]. Figure 12II illustrates the schematic diagram of the initiation and propagation of the pitting corrosion triggered by these composite inclusions in the tested steel. As mentioned above, the mechanisms of galvanic coupling corrosion and the crevice and stress corrosion formed between the inclusions and the matrix could be excluded in this study. Since the composite inclusions are exposed to a corrosive environment with Cl- (Figure 12IIa), CaS with poor stability will preferentially hydrolyze to produce H+ and HS- by anodic reactions (Equations (14) and (15)). As the reaction progresses, the matrix begins to undergo a hydrolysis reaction (Equations (16) and (17)), resulting in further acidification in the pits and further dissolution of CaS [9,14]. The composite inclusion (RE–Zr–Al–Ca)–Ox and the surrounding matrix were gradually dissolved (Equation (18) under the attack of the increasing H+ with the continued dissolution of CaS. Meanwhile, corrosion products produced by cathodic reactions (Equations (19)–(21)) gradually accumulated at the edge of pits (Figure 12 IIb). CaS was completely dissolved, forming and propagating stable pits with the total corrosion products covering the upper layer. To maintain electrical neutrality, a large amount of Cl- will enter the pitting hole, resulting in the formation of a self-catalytic acidification-occluded corrosion cell (Figure 12IIc). Furthermore, some generated cations, such as Ca2+, Zr4+, Al3+ and RE3+, hydrolyze further, resulting in a drop in the pH value [13,30,32], causing a strong acidic environment in the pits and accelerating the dissolution of the composite inclusions ((RE–Zr–Al–Ca)–Ox and Al2MgO4) (Figure 12IId). TiN inclusion is stable, which is primarily related to its insolubility in strong acids [69]. With the dissolution of (RE–Zr–Al–Ca)–Ox, Al2MgO4 and TiN inclusions would fall to the bottom of the pits. The anodic and cathodic reactions involved during the pitting initiation and propagation of the Zr–Ti–Al–RE composite inclusions in the tested steel can be summarized as follows:
p54 The matrix around the spherical inclusions was dissolved to produce cations in the early stage of the pitting corrosion, and Cl− migrated to the anode and diffused in a horizontal direction under the concentration gradient. This resulted in the nearly circular shape of the pits, which eventually produced corrosion products covering the pits [9,13]. Following the formation of the self-catalytic acidification-occluded corrosion cell, the acidic environment accelerates the dissolution of the composite inclusions. However, the findings of the work function analyses of the inclusions in this work demonstrate that the inclusions display different corrosion resistances and the cations exhibit different chemical hydrolysis abilities. These two factors lead to asynchrony of the hydrolysis reaction, resulting in anisotropy of the pitting area around the inclusions.
p56 The inclusions and microstructures in the Zr–Ti–Al–RE deoxidized HSLA steels were modified. The formed spherical Zr–Ti–Al–RE composite inclusions have a core–shell structure with (Zr–Mg–Al–Ca–RE)–Ox at the center and CaS and TiN at the periphery.
p58 The spheroidization and softening effects of rare earth elements on the inclusions can lower the stress concentration around the inclusions and increase the local corrosion resistance of the tested steel. Furthermore, the inclusions containing rare earth elements have a significantly higher work function than the matrix, which further reduces the corrosion tendency of the inclusions to undergo anodic dissolution.
p59 The initiation of pitting corrosion from composite inclusions is triggered by the chemical dissolution of CaS within the composite inclusions. Then, (RE–Zr–Al–Ca)–Ox started to dissolve until the composite inclusion Al2MgO4 region was exposed. Finally, the undissolved Al2MgO4 and TiN fell to the bottom of the pit. The composite spherical inclusions gradually dissolved and transformed into irregular shapes as the immersion duration increased. The addition of rare earth can effectively reduce the tendency for pitting corrosion in a CGHAZ in a marine environment.

### 10.1016/j.jmrt.2025.01.207

### 10.1002/adem.202402545
page1_block1 Exploring the Effect of Lanthanum on the Corrosion Resistance and Antimicrobial Properties of Fe–20Cr–18Ni–6Mo–0.8Cu–0.2N Stainless Steel in Seawater
page1_block4 With adjustments to chemical composition and preparation pro- cess, austenitic stainless steel (SS) has been widely utilized in key component of solid solution-strengthened alloys.[1] This iron- based alloy primarily consists of chromium (Cr) and nickel (Ni), with Cr content generally exceeding 16% and Ni content
page1_block5 typically above 8%. Super austenitic stainless steel (SASS), characterized by the addition of high-alloying elements such as molybdenum (Mo) and Ni, exhibits superior corrosion resistance and enhanced mechanical properties compared to conventional austenitic SS. These improvements make them suitable for demanding environmental conditions.[2,3]
page1_block6 For example, 904L SASS demonstrates excellent corrosion resistance in high- temperature, high-concentration sulfuric acid environments. However, its corrosion resistance varies with changes in tempera- ture and sulfuric acid concentration.[4–6]
page1_block7 Moreover, aging treatments signiﬁcantly inﬂuence the corrosion resistance of SASS, with appropriate aging treatment further enhancing its performance.[7]
page1_block8 Similarly, heat treatment of 316L SS at 1200 °C reduces the number of grain boundaries while inducing the formation of Σ3 twin boundaries. This process effec- tively prevents the precipitation of harmful phases, thereby improving the material’s corrosion resistance.[8]
page1_block9 The corrosion resistance of SASS is primarily inﬂuenced by its high content of alloying elements such as Cr, Ni, Mo, and others. The Cr in these steels promotes the formation of a Cr2O3 passive ﬁlm on the material’s surface, effectively protecting it from chloride ion (Cl) corrosion in seawater environments.[9,10]
page1_block17 This study investigates the effects of lanthanum (La) on the microstructure and corrosion resistance of Fe–20Cr–18Ni–6Mo–0.8Cu stainless steel in seawater. Microstructural analysis shows that La addition reﬁnes grain size and alters precipitated phases. X-ray diffraction conﬁrms austenitic structures, while transmission electron microscopy reveals the formation of Cu5La compounds and ferrite/σ phases. Electrochemical tests indicate that the sample without La has the highest open-circuit potential and best corrosion resistance in nonseawater conditions. However, after 16 days of seawater exposure, the 0.5 wt% La sample exhibits superior corrosion resistance with a corrosion rate of 0.0175 mm/a, while higher La contents (1.0 wt% and 1.5 wt%) leads to poor corrosion resistance and large corrosion craters. Scanning electron microscopy conﬁrms minimal surface corrosion for the 0.5 wt% La sample. In sulfate-reducing bacteria environments, La enhances corrosion resistance, except for pitting corrosion observed in the 1.5 wt% sample. Scanning Kelvin probe force microscopy shows minimal surface potential ﬂuctuation (from 30 to 10 mV) for the 0 wt% La sample, indicating the best corrosion resistance. This study provides insights into the role of rare earth elements in super austenitic stainless steels.
page2_block1 Ni expands the austenite phase region, facilitating the formation of a stable austenite microstructure. An increase in Ni content enhances austenite precipitation, growth, and overall proportion. Promoting the formation of a single austenite, thereby improv- ing the microstructure of the material.[11,12] Mo is a critical alloy- ing element for enhancing the corrosion resistance of austenitic SS, as it improves the passivation of the material.[13–15] In highly Cr environments, such as 3.5% NaCl solutions, an increase in Mo content contributes to the corrosion resistance of the material, because Mo can promote the formation of surface pas- sive ﬁlm Cr2O3, signiﬁcantly improve the corrosion resistance of SS.[16–18] However, Mo segregation and σ-phase precipitation can directly affect the corrosion resistance of SASS. Adding cop- per (Cu) serves dual purposes: improving cold deformability and accelerating the formation of a dense passive ﬁlm in stainless steel. This enhances resistance to uniform corrosion, pitting cor- rosion, and crevice corrosion.[19–22] The inclusion of nitrogen (N) further stabilizes the austenite phase. At a N content of 1.2%, the matrix transitions to a single austenite phase.[23,24] This enhances the stability of the passive ﬁlm, thereby improves localized corrosion resistance, particularly against pitting and crevice corrosion.[25–27]
page2_block2 The addition of rare earth elements (REEs) to SS signiﬁcantly impacts its inclusions, effectively reﬁning the grain size of austenitic SS, reducing the number of primary carbides, and improving its corrosion resistance.[28–31] Among REEs, cerium (Ce) and its compounds exhibit strong antimicrobial, bacteri- cidal, and anti-inﬂammatory properties.[32–34] Studies have shown that incorporating Ce and Cu into duplex stainless steel 2205-Cu–Ce inhibits the formation of sulfate-reducing bacteria (SRB) bioﬁlms and enhances resistance to hydrogen-induced cracking due to hydrogen trapping by Cu-rich precipitates, thereby improving corrosion resistance.[35,36] Luo et al. reported that adding 0.15–0.20 wt% Ce resulted in optimal corrosion resis- tance, while further increases in Ce content led to a gradual decline in performance.[37]
page2_block3 Another REE, lanthanum (La), inﬂuences the microstructure of super austenitic stainless steel by reﬁning grains, altering inclusions, improving mechanical and corrosion resistance, and enhancing solidiﬁcation microstructure, thus contributing to superior corrosion resistance.[38,39] In low Ni–Cr–Mn–N SS, appropriate levels of La can reduce twin crystal formation and reﬁne the microstructure, resulting in improved corrosion resis- tance in simulated seawater environments.[40] Similarly, in 1Cr13 SS, samples subjected to quenching and tempering with La exhibited better corrosion resistance compared to those without La.[41] In steels with high niobium (Nb) content, the addition of La promotes the solid solubility of the second-phase Nb (C, N) particles and lowers the solution temperature, thereby aiding in controlling austenite grain growth.[42]
page2_block4 An appropriate amount REEs in SS can signiﬁcantly enhance corrosion resistance and antimicrobial properties. For instance, the addition of La to 316L SS improves its antimicrobial proper- ties.[43] Similarly, incorporating a small amount of REE yttrium (Y) enhances corrosion resistance by modifying inclusions.[44]
page2_block5 Additionally, REEs have been shown to improve the corrosion resistance of microalloyed steel in a 3.5 wt% NaCl solution.[45]
page2_block6 However, the effects of La addition on the corrosion resistance and microstructure of Fe–20Cr–18Ni–6Mo–0.8Cu–0.2N stainless
page2_block7 steel remain underexplored. In this study, ﬁve SASS samples with varying La contents (Fe–20Cr–18Ni–6Mo–0.8Cu–0.2–xLa, where x = 0, 0.5, 1.0, 1.5, 2.0 wt%) were prepared. The microstructures, electrochemical properties, and seawater corrosion resistance of these samples were systematically investigated.
page2_block10 Fe–20Cr–18Ni–6Mo–0.8Cu–0.2N–xLa (x = 0, 0.5, 1.0, 1.5, 2.0 wt%), a highly corrosion resistant SASS, was prepared using a melting furnace. Raw materials such as ferroalloys, Cr, Ni, Mo, and La were ﬁrst strictly inspected and pretreated before being placed into crucibles for vacuum induction smelting. This process involved evacuating the crucible to create a vacuum and heating it to 1550–1570 °C to melt the materials. Vacuum smelting effec- tively removes impurities, enhances SS purity, and facilitates the introduction of nitrogen into the molten state. During this process, N was injected and stirred to ensure a homogeneous composition, known as vapor-phase N enrichment, this step signiﬁcantly improves the strength and corrosion resistance of the SS.
page2_block11 Subsequently, the steel underwent out-of-hearth reﬁning to control the N content and ensure its cleanliness and quality. The temperature was then reduced to 1430–1490 °C for casting. During casting, the material was cooled and molded while main- taining a vacuum pressure of 0.07–0.08 MPa to prevent slag for- mation. Additional steps included mold release, ingot heating, hot forging, surface cleaning, and rounding to ensure dimen- sional accuracy and surface quality.
page2_block12 Finally, the material was subjected to homogenization anneal- ing and high-temperature heat treatment to eliminate composi- tional segregation and σ-phase precipitation formed during the previous process. Fine control of the temperature and atmo- sphere conditions throughout the melting process was critical to achieving the high quality and excellent performance of the ﬁnal product. The resulting Fe–20Cr–18Ni–6Mo–0.8Cu–0.2N–xLa (x = 0, 0.5, 1.0, 1.5, 2.0 wt%) SASS was then analyzed for its spe- ciﬁc composition using inductively coupled plasma atomic emis- sion spectroscopy (ICP-AES), as shown in Table 1.
page2_block13 2.2. Metallographic Preparation and Analysis
page2_block14 The Fe–20Cr–18Ni–6Mo–0.8Cu–0.2N–xLa alloy samples under- went a meticulous preparation process to ensure a high-quality surface for metallographic analysis. Samples were ﬁrst cut into standardized sizes and shapes, followed by mechanical polishing steps using progressively ﬁner grades of sandpaper, starting at 800 grit and ﬁnishing with 3000 grit. Each polishing step was executed with a ﬁgure-eight motion to prevent cross-hatch pat- terns, and the samples were cleaned with a solvent between each grit change to remove debris that could affect subsequent polish- ing. After the ﬁnal polishing, the samples were ultrasonically cleaned in an alcohol bath to remove any residual polishing com- pounds. Etching was carried out using a freshly prepared aqua regia solution mixed in a 3:1 ratio of hydrochloric acid to nitric acid. The samples were immersed for precisely 5 s, a duration
page3_block1 optimized in preliminary tests to balance etching effectiveness with prevention of over-etching. Bubble formation on the sample surfaces indicated the etching reaction, which was then promptly halted by rinsing in alcohol. This step was crucial for preserving the microstructure without further etching. The samples were gently blown dry with a cool, dry air stream to avoid water spots or marks that could interfere with subsequent microscopic exam- ination. The etched samples were examined using a ZEISS Vert A1 metallographic microscope, which provided high-resolution imaging of the etched microstructure. The microscope, equipped with a range of objectives, allowed for detailed observation at various magniﬁcations, with images captured using a high- resolution digital camera.
page3_block3 The surface morphology of the samples was examined using a Regulus 8100 ﬁeld-emission SEM, known for its high-resolution imaging capabilities. The SEM scans the sample surface with a ﬁne electron beam, which interacts with the material to produce secondary electrons that generate detailed surface images. Samples were mounted on SEM stubs using a conductive adhesive and coated with a thin layer of gold to ensure electrical conductivity and prevent charging effects during imaging. The SEM was operated at accelerating voltages suitable for the desired magniﬁcations, capturing images at 100, 200, 500, 1000 to reveal various levels of surface detail. In addition to SEM imag- ing, EDS was performed to analyze the elemental composition of the samples. EDS detects X-rays emitted by the sample when excited by the electron beam. The EDS detector (Model 550i) captured X-rays emitted by the sample under electron beam exci- tation, with data collected over a broad energy range to ensure the detection of all relevant elements.
page3_block5 XRD analysis was conducted to determine the crystalline phases present in the alloy samples. The samples were carefully mounted on a goniometer head, allowing precise control of sam- ple orientation during the XRD scan. The analysis covered a 2θ range from 10 to 80°, using a high-intensity X-ray source and a high-resolution detector. Diffraction patterns were collected as a function of the diffraction angle, and the data was processed to identify characteristic peaks corresponding to different crystal- line phases. The XRD data was analyzed by comparing the
page3_block6 observed peak positions and intensities with known phases from a standard diffraction database, identifying the phases present in the alloy and their relative amounts. This analysis provided valu- able insights into the crystallographic structure of the material, which is intrinsically linked to its mechanical and chemical properties.
page3_block8 TEM was employed to investigate the internal structure of the alloy samples at the atomic level. Sample preparation involved grinding the samples into thin foils, which were subsequently punched into discs using a microtome. Further thinning was achieved using a twin-jet polisher to make the samples electron transparent. The thinned samples were then mounted on TEM grids for analysis. The TEM, operating at a high accelerating voltage, provided high-resolution imaging of the sample. The selected area electron diffraction (SAED) mode was employed to obtain diffraction patterns from speciﬁc areas of the sample, revealing information about the crystal structure and the presence of defects such as dislocations or grain bound- aries. The combined TEM and SAED analyses were instrumental in understanding the microstructural features that govern the material’s mechanical and chemical properties.
page3_block9 2.6. Electrochemical Testing
page3_block10 The electrochemical behavior of the alloy samples was evaluated through a series of tests conducted in natural seawater. A poten- tiostat was used to control the power output via a potential con- troller, ensuring the electrochemical reactions proceeded at the desired potential. Samples were mounted in a custom-designed electrochemical cell, employing a saturated calomel electrode as the reference electrode and a platinum counter electrode. The open circuit potential (OCP) test monitored the potential of the samples as they equilibrated in seawater, while electro- chemical impedance spectroscopy (EIS) test applied a small amplitude sinusoidal voltage to measure the current response, providing data on the samples’ impedance and corrosion resis- tance. The polarization curve (Tafel) test involved applying a linearly increasing potential to the samples and measuring the resulting current. The Tafel plot, showing current density versus potential, was used to determine the corrosion potential and current, key parameters for evaluating corrosion resistance. The volume of seawater used in the test was 300 mL. The Parstat 4000þ equipment controlled the potential and measured
page3_block11 Table 1. Chemical composition of Fe–20Cr–18Ni–6Mo–0.8Cu–0.2N–xLa prepared by vacuum melting.
page4_block1 the current, with data collected and analyzed using specialized electrochemical software.
page4_block3 Designed to simulate long-term exposure, this experiment required the samples to be polished with a 1.0 μm diamond polish. The rotational speed was set at 300 r min1 during the mechanical polishing process, and the polishing time was 3 min each time. The surface of the polished samples was presented to a mirror effect to obtain consistent initial conditions. Three par- allel samples of each alloy composition were suspended vertically in a 500 mL beaker for uniform exposure. The seawater used for the experiment was pure natural seawater without ﬁltering treat- ment. Samples were retrieved at intervals for assessment of weight, morphology, and surface corrosion. Weight loss was measured with a high-precision balance, and surface morphology was examined with a stereo microscope, documenting corrosion devel- opment through photography. Post-immersion, samples were cleaned to remove loose corrosion products before SEM examina- tion to observe the microscopic morphology of corroded surfaces. SEM images detailed the type and extent of corrosion, including corrosion products and the underlying metal surface morphology.
page4_block4 2.8. SRB Corrosion Experiment
page4_block5 Desulfovibrio caledoniensis is a species of SRB known to conserve energy for growth through electron transfer to insoluble electron acceptors.[44] The corrosion solution was prepared by adding 1 mL of the strain to 100 mL of SRB culture medium. Three par- allel samples were placed in anaerobic bottles containing the SRB culture medium, which was Postgate’s C medium with the fol- lowing composition: 0.3 g L1 sodium citrate, 6 mL L1 sodium lactate, 1 g L1 NH4CL, 0.06 g L1 CaCl2–6H2O, 0.06 g L1
page4_block6 MgSO4, 0.5 g L1 KH2PO4, and 1000 mL seawater.[46] The pH of the corrosive solution was adjusted to 7.20  0.05 using NaOH solution. The solution was then deoxygenated for 30 min with high-purity N2 gas, and disposable needle ﬁlters with a pore size of 0.22 μm were used to prevent contamination, set the stirring speed to 150 r min1. No yellow rust was observed in the solution of the blank control group, indicating effective deoxygenation. The culture medium was autoclaved at 121 °C for 20 min to sterilize it.[47] The anaerobic bottles were sterilized under ultraviolet light on a clean bench for 30 min to prevent bacterial contamination from the air. Then, injected into the SRB, put them in a thermostat at 30 °C for 7 and 21 days. To ensure maximum SRB survival, the dissolved oxygen concentra- tion was maintained below 40 ppb after deoxygenation. The anti- microbial properties of the materials were assessed by staining live cells with acridine orange (OA) for 10 min after 7 days, which appeared green under a ﬂuorescence microscope, counting the SRB numbers using hemocytometer, and observing the corro- sion characteristics of the material surface after 21 days.
page4_block8 Scanning Kelvin probe force microscopy (SKPFM) was used to examine the nanoscale surface morphology and potential of the
page4_block9 alloy samples. Samples were mounted on glass slides with a non- conductive adhesive, ensuring SKPFM tip contact. Operating in tapping mode, the SKPFM’s cantilever with a sharp tip oscillated over the sample surface, generating topographical images from oscillation amplitude changes. Equipped with a conductive tip, the SKPFM also measured surface potential, providing informa- tion on local electronic property variations related to phase pres- ence or corrosion product formation. Data were collected over a 50 μm  50 μm area, offering a detailed view of surface features and their distribution.
page4_block11 After 16 days of seawater immersion, surface roughness testing was performed to evaluate changes caused by corrosion. The Samples were mounted on a laser confocal microscope, which utilized a laser beam to probe the surface and measure reﬂected light. The 50 objective lens provided a high-resolution imaging of surface topography. Three locations on each sample were tested for roughness to ensure a representative assessment of surface roughness. The confocal microscope captured images at each location, generating three-dimensional surface topogra- phy maps. These maps detailed surface roughness parameters such as arithmetic mean height, root mean square roughness, and maximum height, essential for understanding the seawater immersion’s effect on the alloy samples’ surface condition and its inﬂuence on corrosion resistance and overall performance.
page4_block15 Figure 2 shows the metallographic (200 and 1000) and SEM (500 and 1000) of Fe–20Cr–18Ni–6Mo–0.8Cu–0.2N–xLa (x = 0, 0.5, 1.0, 1.5, 2.0 wt%). From Figure 2a1,a2, it is evident that discontinuous second-phase precipitates appear along the grain boundaries without the addition of the La element. These precipitates are primarily skeletal, with some thin stripes and lump-like structures and EDS analysis indicates that these precipitates are enriched in Mo. After the addition of 0.5 wt% La, Figure 2b1,b2 shows that the austenite grains in the matrix undergo signiﬁcant reﬁnement, resulting in a denser microstruc- ture. This dense network structure acts as a barrier to corrosion, preventing it from one α grain to another. The diffuse distribu- tion of La within the matrix contributes grain reﬁnement, as La puriﬁes the grain boundaries and reduces the segregation of matrix elements. EDS results conﬁrm that Cr, Mo, and Ni are uniformly distributed within the matrix at this La content. When the La content is increased to 1.0 and 1.5 wt%, Figure 2c1,d1 reveals that the matrix microstructure becomes less compact, with an increase in second-phase precipitates along the grain boundaries. This is due to the excessive La additions, which lead to the aggregation and inhomogeneous distribution of inclu- sions. These changes negatively impact the material’s corrosion resistance. At 2.0 wt% La addition, as shown in Figure 2e1, the grain reﬁnement effect becomes more pronounced. Dense and well-deﬁned grain boundaries are observed over short ranges, with
page5_block1 more complete and clearer proﬁles. Additionally, the second-phase precipitates appear uniformly distributed within the matrix, fur- ther enhancing the material’s microstructural integrity.
page5_block2 3.2. Physical Analysis
page5_block3 Figure 3 shows the XRD diffraction patterns of Fe–20Cr–18Ni– 6Mo–0.8Cu–0.2N alloys with varying amounts of the La element. The intensity and number of diffraction peaks provide insights into the inﬂuence of La on the alloy’s microstructure. For the sample without La addition, the diffraction peaks are relatively simple, with a prominent peak corresponding to the (321) crystal plane. Comparison with standard reference cards indicates that the primary phase of the microstructure is Fe–Cr–Mo. With the addition of La, the diffraction patterns change signiﬁcantly. In the 0.5 wt% La sample, a strong peak corresponding to the Cr0.19Fe0.7Ni0.11 austenite matrix is observed. This behavior is attributed to La’s ability to reﬁne the matrix microstructure, alter the composition and morphology of inclusions, and thereby inﬂuence the intensity and position of the diffraction peaks. As the La content increases to 1.0, 1.5, and 2.0 wt%, additional diffraction peaks appear, reﬂecting the more complex micro- structure introduced by higher La levels. These changes suggest that La additions affect the crystallographic structure by modify- ing the distribution and characteristics of inclusions in the alloy.
page5_block4 In addition to the austenite matrix peaks, phases of Cr3MoNi2 were also observed, with the intensity of these diffraction peaks increasing as the La element increased. Higher diffraction peak intensity indicates stronger diffraction from the corresponding crystal plane or a greater abundance of that crystal plane in the sample. This suggests that La has a signiﬁcant impact on the material’s properties, leading to a denser microstructure and an increased number of speciﬁc phases. The strong chemical activity of REEs like La allows them to form stable intermetallic compounds with the transition metal, promoting the formation
page5_block5 of Cr3MoNi2 phases.[48] Additionally, the diffraction patterns of 1.5 and 2.0 wt% La samples reveal numerous tiny peaks and waves. These small peaks are primarily due to La elemental dif- fraction, occurring at the (111), (222), and (331) crystal planes. The addition of La not only improves the material’s microstruc- ture but also facilitates the formation of speciﬁc phases by reduc- ing atomic mismatch and optimizing chemical potential.[49]
page5_block6 This highlights the crucial role of La in enhancing the crystallo- graphic and microstructural properties of the alloy.
page5_block7 3.3. Precipitated Phases Analysis
page5_block8 TEM analysis was conducted to investigate the microstructure, chemical composition, and crystal structure of the material, as shown in Figure 4. The TEM plots of area diffraction were selected for different samples, revealing the presence of ferrite Fe–Cr precipitation in the 0 wt% samples, in SASS, ferrite typically appears as ﬂakes or islands.[50] From the microarea com- position analysis of the 0 wt% in Figure 4a indicated that the Cr content was 59.48 at%, and Ni content was 32.26 at%, conﬁrming the precipitation of ferrite phases. Figure 4b shows a black region enriched with elemental Ni, and the elemental distribution scan in this area highlighted the presence of La and Ni, conﬁrming the formation of Ni–La phases. As shown in Figure 4c, intermetallic compounds with a cubic crystal structure were observed, with Cu being the primary element (44.38%), while La content reached 9.81%. This is attributed to La’s role in promoting the formation of the Cu5La phase by reducing atomic mismatches and optimiz- ing chemical potential. Figure 4d illustrates the precipitation of the σ phase. EDS analysis of the labeled region revealed a high Cr content, with Mo and Ni contents being nearly equal, further validating the precipitation of the σ phase. Additionally, La- and Ni-rich precipitated phases were identiﬁed in the energy spec- trum analysis and EDS distribution shown in Figure 4e.
page7_block1 3.4. Electrochemical Analysis of Seawater Corrosion at 0 and 16 days
page7_block2 The OCP results indicated the material’s tendency toward corro- sion and provided insights into its corrosion behavior in seawa- ter. As shown in Figure 5a, the corrosion tendency of the material increased with the La content. After 16 days of seawater immer- sion, the OCP shifted positively, as depicted in Figure 5b. For instance, the OCP of the 0.5 wt% sample increased from 0.301 to 0.167 V, while the 0 wt% sample increased from 0.234 to 0.120 V. This indicated the formation of a thicker passive ﬁlm on the matrix surface, reducing the corrosion tendency. The more signiﬁcant OCP increase in the 0.5 wt% sample was due to La acting as a catalyst for the formation of Cr and Mo oxides, which contributed to passive ﬁlm development. However, the poor thermodynamic stability of rare-earth oxides meant that La was not stably retained in the passive ﬁlm.[51] Additionally, the reduced ﬂuctuations in the OCP curves of the 1.5 and 2.0 wt% samples were attributed to a balance between Cl-induced matrix corrosion and the protective effect of the passive ﬁlm.
page7_block3 EIS measurements after 0 and 16 days of seawater immersion, shown in Figure 5c,d, demonstrated an increase in impedance over time. The arc radius of the high-frequency region followed the order: 0 > 0.5 > 2.0 > 1.5 > 1.0 wt%. A larger arc radius at the high frequencies indicated that greater difﬁculty in charge trans- fer between the sample surface and the corrosive medium, sug- gesting better corrosion resistance for the 0 and 0.5 wt% samples.
page7_block4 Tafel curves in Figure 5e revealed that, before corrosion, the 0 and 0.5 wt% samples exhibited similar behavior, indicating that their corrosion mechanisms were comparable. Both showed an incomplete passive zone, likely because EIS cannot induce pas- sivation on the electrode surface, whereas anodic passivation occurred during polarization. The distinct composition of the 0.5 wt% sample caused certain components to passivate more easily during electrolysis, while others remained highly active, resulting in an incomplete passive state similar to that of the 0 wt% sample. In contrast, samples with higher La content displayed clear passive zones in their polarization curves. Furthermore, the self-corrosion potential of uncorroded samples decreased with increasing La content.
page7_block5 The bode plot was be used to describe the dynamic character- istics of the system. In general, the higher the impedance, the better the corrosion resistance of the sample’s ﬁlm layer. As shown in Figure 5g, the system with the 0 wt% sample exhibited the highest total impedance. The maximum phase angle for 1.0 and 1.5 wt% samples was close to 40°, indicating noticeably poor corrosion performance. Furthermore, the phase angles of these samples overlapped in the high-frequency region, suggesting that their corrosion rates were similar.
page7_block6 3.5. SEM Analysis after Corrosion in Seawater
page7_block7 The corrosion of metal materials in seawater is highly signiﬁcant due to the presence of microorganisms, salt, and other corrosive factors. Figure 6a illustrates the macroscopic morphology of the samples after exposure to natural seawater. After 1 day of immer- sion, signs of corrosion were observed only on the surface of the 1.5 wt% sample. By the second day, slight corrosion appeared on the surface of the 1.0 wt% sample, while corrosion on the 1.5 wt% sample intensiﬁed. On the fourth day, no signiﬁcant corrosion was detected on the surfaces of the other samples. Observations of the samples on the 8th, 12th, and 16th days revealed no noticeable macroscopic corrosion behavior in the 0, 0.5, and 2.0 wt% samples. However, the 1.5 wt% sample exhib- ited a larger corrosion area, with a darker rust color and localized regions where the rust layer thickened. This increased corrosion is likely due to the corrosion products of 1.5 wt% had more pre- cipitated phases, which signiﬁcantly impaired the material’s cor- rosion resistance. As shown in the SEM images from prior studies, the microstructure of 1.5 wt% sample was sparse, leading to easy corrosion between adjacent grains. Surface defects in this material were also pronounced, exacerbating its vulnerability. The Cl in the seawater readily penetrated the passive ﬁlm on the material’s surface, causing severe corrosion.
page7_block8 To further understand the corrosion behavior, the weight of each sample was measured before and after corrosion, as shown in Figure 6b. The corrosion rate was determined as the average of three weight measurements taken before and after corrosion for each sample. The calculation was performed using Equation (1):
page9_block1 Figure 5. Electrochemical test plots of Fe–20Cr–18Ni–6Mo–0.8Cu–0.2N–La alloys. The a,b) OCP; c,d) EIS; e,f) Tafel curves; and g,h) Bode plot of Fe–20Cr–18Ni–6Mo–0.8Cu–0.2N–La alloys after 0 and 16 days of seawater corrosion.
page10_block3 where v is the average corrosion rate (mm a1); ΔW is the sam- ples weight loss (mg); A is the total surface area (cm2); ρ is the density of austenitic stainless steel (g cm3); and t is the experi- mental time (h).
page10_block4 Based on the surface area of the sample (14 cm2), the density of austenitic stainless steel (7.93 g cm3), and the seawater immersion time of 384 h, the calculated corrosion rates were as follows: 0 wt%: 0.0109 mm a1, 0.5 wt%: 0.0175 mm a1, 1.0 wt%: 0.0309 mm a1, 1.5 wt%: 0.0358 mm a1, and 2.0 wt%: 0.0211 mm a1. The average corrosion weight loss rate indicated that the 1.5 wt% sample experienced the most signiﬁcant corro- sion, followed by 1.0 wt% sample. In contrast, the others exhib- ited a smaller corrosion weight loss rate, aligning with the macroscopic surface morphology analysis after seawater immer- sion. Figure 6c presents a histogram of the average corrosion rate, with the error bars conﬁrming the accuracy of the experi- mental results.
page10_block5 Figure 7a shows SEM image of the samples after seawater corrosion. Numerous pitting pits were observed on the material surfaces, forming through three main stages: 1) the rupture of the passive ﬁlm initiated pitting nucleation; 2) pits continued to
page10_block6 grow; and 3) larger corrosion pits ultimately formed. The mech- anism is illustrated in Figure 7b.[52,53]
page10_block7 On the 0 wt% surface, the number of corrosion pits was relatively small. These pits were triangular, with shallow corro- sion depths at the edges and deeper centers. For the 0.5 wt% sample, corrosion pits increased, and small corrosion cracks appeared in speciﬁc areas, extending in various directions. This phenomenon was primarily related to defects and surface scratches. The 1.0 wt% surface exhibited numerous small corrosion pits, but the corrosion did not spread signiﬁcantly from the pits’ centers. The 1.5 wt% sample had shallower pits than other samples, but its corrosion range and pit sizes were larger. Notably, circular corrosion pits appeared, likely due to the accumulation of hazardous materials on the sample. The corro- sion resistance of granular second-phase regions was lower, and corrosion tended to initiate preferentially in these areas. Although the 2.0 wt% sample exhibited fewer corrosion pits, the size of the pits was larger. Additionally, more corrosion products were attached to its surface, which inﬂuenced the results of the corrosion loss experiment. Based on a comprehen- sive analysis of the experimental results, it was concluded that the 1.0 and 1.5 wt% samples had the poorest corrosion resis- tance. In contrast, the 0, 0.5, and 2.0 wt% samples demonstrated better corrosion resistance.
page10_block8 Figure 6. Natural seawater corrosion analysis of Fe–20Cr–18Ni–6Mo–0.8Cu–0.2N–La alloys. a) Corrosion morphology, b) rate of weight loss, and c) average corrosion rate.
page11_block2 The corrosion of SS by SRB occurs primarily carried out through the formation of bioﬁlm, electron exchange, electron exchange, and chemical reactions, with fast corrosion rate and high biological activity.[54,55] SRB metabolizes sulfate (SO4
page11_block3 2) to sulﬁde (S2) or H2S, which could further react with metal ions to form metal sulﬁdes, signiﬁcantly contributing to metal corro- sion.[56,57] In SRB environments, copper-containing alloys
page11_block4 initially exhibit a certain degree of inhibitory effect on SRB growth and metabolism due to the toxicity of copper ions and the formation of a passive oxide layer on the alloy surface. However, once SRB produce hydrogen sulﬁde (H2S), the surface of the copper-containing alloy undergoes a transformation, lead- ing to the formation of copper sulﬁdes (such as CuS and Cu2S). These copper sulﬁdes do not provide protective properties; instead, they promote localized corrosion. Therefore, while copper may initially inhibit SRB activity, its initial
page11_block5 Figure 7. a) SEM images of Fe–20Cr–18Ni–6Mo–0.8Cu–0.2N–La alloys after 16 days of seawater corrosion, b) schematic diagram of pitting formation, and c) corrosion mechanism diagram of SRB.
page12_block1 suppressive effect diminishes in the presence of H2S, potentially reversing into an accelerative effect that promotes rather than inhibits the corrosion process.[34,58,59] The addition of La enhances material performance by purifying grain boundaries and altering inclusion morphology, as illustrated in Figure 7c.[60,61]
page12_block2 Figure 8a shows the results of live cell staining with AO, indi- cating that the number of cells in the solution decreases as the La content increases. As shown in Figure 8b, after 7 days of growth, the SRB count in the medium was 4.64  108 cells mL1. The SRB population gradually decreased with increasing La content, demonstrating that the sample exhibited an inhibitory effect on SRB growth. When the La content reached 2.0 wt%, the SRB count was reduced to 6.40  107 cells mL1. The SRB exper- imental procedure was carried out as described previously, with the main steps summarized in Figure 9a. Figure 9b shows the
page12_block3 surface morphology of samples subjected SRB corrosion. Sample with 1.5 wt% La experienced the most severe corrosion, whereas other samples exhibited fewer corrosion products. SEM images (Figure 9c) revealed that the 0 wt% sample had larger, shallow corrosion pits and small cracks. The 0.5 wt% sample showed larger pits, with small corrosion spots and cracks forming between them. In contrast, the 1.0 and 1.5 wt% samples dis- played smaller, predominantly pitting corrosion, accompanied by a thick layer of corrosion products. These products, formed via SRB-mediated sulfate reduction to sulﬁde, exhibited cracks and large corrosion pits. This phenomenon can be attributed to electron exchange between SRB and the metal substrate under the bioﬁlm, which promotes sulﬁde deposition, increases poten- tial differences, and accelerates corrosion.[62,63] The 2.0 wt% sam- ple displayed minimal corrosion, with fewer craters and a comparatively smooth surface.
page13_block2 According to the three-dimensional (3D) surface morphology shown in Figure 10a, the 0 wt% sample exhibited the smallest
page13_block4 Figure 9. a) Experimental ﬂowchart, b) corrosion morphology, and c) SEM images after SRB-induced corrosion of Fe–20Cr–18Ni–6Mo–0.8Cu–0.2N–La alloys.
page14_block1 height ﬂuctuation curves revealed that the 0 wt% sample had the smallest height ﬂuctuation range, between 20 and 90 nm. In this sample, the precipitated phases were uniformly distributed within the matrix, with a signiﬁcant number localized at grain
page14_block2 boundaries. The 1.0 wt% sample exhibited a maximum height ﬂuctuation of 80–100 nm, with fewer precipitated phases at grain boundaries, though their sizes were unevenly distributed.

page15_block2 (Figure 10c) and potential ﬂuctuation plots (Figure 10e) demon- strated that the surface potential at grain boundaries was signiﬁ- cantly lower than within the matrix, suggesting that corrosion preferentially occurred at grain boundaries. The 0 wt% sample

page16_block1 showed minimal potential ﬂuctuation, ranging was 30–10 mV, while the 1.5 wt% sample exhibited the largest ﬂuctuation, 60–40 mV, indicating a higher corrosion tendency at the grain boundaries of the latter. In the 2.0 wt% sample, fewer precipi- tated phases were observed at the grain boundaries, and the corresponding surface potential ﬂuctuation was relatively small. For the 0.5 and 1.5 wt% samples, surface potential ﬂuctuation data revealed that the presence of precipitated phases increased the potential difference across the matrix, thereby enhancing the corrosion tendency.
page16_block2 These ﬁndings suggest that the addition of La inﬂuences sur- face potential by altering the size, distribution, and number of precipitated phases, thereby modifying the corrosion tendency of the matrix.
page16_block4 Figures 11 and 12 show the surface roughness information for different content of La samples in seawater corrosion for 16 days. As shown in Figure 12, at 0 wt%, corrosion increases the surface
page16_block5 Figure 13. Corrosion resistance mechanisms of Fe–20Cr–18Ni–6Mo–0.8Cu–0.2N–La alloys.
page17_block1 roughness by 0.052 μm, indicating the best corrosion resistance. In contrast, at 1.5 wt%, corrosion-induced roughness increases by 0.236 μm, reﬂecting the worst corrosion resistance. Seawater corrosion for 16 days, the roughness of the 0 wt% is compara- tively <0.5 wt%. However, the range of surface ﬂuctuations shown in Figure 11 (0.5 La) indicated that the 0.5 wt% sample surface was smoother than the 0 wt%. This was attributable to the 0.5 wt% surface had more severe uniform corrosion. The main reason for the roughness of 0 wt% is smaller than 0.5 wt% is that the surface corrosion depth of 0.5 wt% is larger. The surface roughness increased when the La content was increased to 1.0 and 1.5 wt%. Figure 11 (1.0 La) shows that the maximum ﬂuctuation of the 1.0 wt% surface is from 9.807 to 8.393 μm. This indicated the corrosion on the surface of the mate- rial was not uniform. Figure 12 shows that the 1.5 wt% sample had the largest roughness after corrosion and the most severe corro- sion. It is noteworthy that although the roughness of 2.0 wt% is smaller than that of 1.0 and 1.5 wt%, there is severe corrosion below the reference surface as shown by the range of surface ﬂuc- tuation of 2.0 wt% sample. This indicated that excessive additions of La could cause more severe corrosion of the material.
page17_block2 3.9. Mechanism Analysis of Lanthanum Effect on Corrosion Resistance
page17_block3 The experimental results presented in the previous section pro- vide insights into the nucleation process and corrosion mecha- nism of Fe–20Cr–18Ni–6Mo–0.8Cu–0.2N–xLa alloys (x = 0, 0.5, 1.0, 1.5, 2.0 wt%), as illustrated in Figure 13. For the alloy without added REEs, the grain size was relatively large, allowing Cl to penetrate adjacent grains easily, resulting in signiﬁcant corro- sion. Additionally, numerous inclusions and precipitated phases within the grains contributed to poor corrosion resistance. In contrast, the addition of the La during high-temperature melt- ing effectively reﬁned the crystal structure and improved the alloy’s microstructure. This improvement enhanced corrosion resistance through the formation of high-melting-point rare earth inclusions, such as La2O3, La2O2S, etc., which act as heterogeneous nucleation sites. When 0.5 wt% La was added, grain reﬁnement occurred as La was uniformly distributed within the crystal, reducing harmful inclusions and suppressing the precipitation of secondary phases. The aggregation of La at grain boundaries increased surface energy, effectively preventing Cl and SRB-induced corrosion.
page17_block4 At 1.0 wt% La, the alloy’s microstructure was further reﬁned, and Cl-induced corrosion was signiﬁcantly inhibited. Additionally, La enriched the inner oxide ﬁlm on the substrate’s surface. The microstructure and corrosion resistance of the alloy deteriorated when the La content reached 1.5 wt%. Excessive La led to the formation of a large number of alloying compounds during solidiﬁcation, releasing latent heat of crystallization and reducing the subcooling of the liquid metal. This weakened the grain reﬁnement effect, resulting in coarser grains and increased secondary phase precipitation, thereby exacerbating Cl and SRB-induced corrosion. Conversely, the 2.0 wt% La alloy displayed a more homogeneous microstructure with fewer inclu- sions, exhibiting improved corrosion resistance compared to the 1.5 wt% alloy.
page17_block6 The effects of varying REE La contents on the SASS Fe–20Cr– 18Ni–6Mo–0.8Cu–0.2N–xLa (x = 0, 0.5, 1.0, 1.5, 2.0 wt%) were investigated, and the following conclusions were drawn: 1) the addition of La signiﬁcantly inﬂuenced the microstructure of the alloy. XRD and TEM analyses revealed that the SASS matrix exhibited a Cr0.19Fe0.7Ni0.11 austenitic structure. In the 0 wt% sample, the primary precipitated phase was Fe–Cr–Mo-based ferrite, whereas σ-phase precipitation was observed in the 0.5 and 1.0 wt% samples. Additionally, Cu5La and La–Ni phases were detected, along with Ni-enriched regions, as evident from elemental distribution plots; 2) electrochemical tests indicated that the 0 wt% sample had the highest OCP without corrosion. After 16 days of seawater exposure, the 0.5 wt% sample exhibited the best corrosion resistance, followed by the 0 wt% sample. In contrast, the 1.5 wt% sample displayed the poorest corrosion resistance, with large corrosion pits observed on its surface. In an SRB environment, La has certain antimicrobial properties, the 0 and 0.5 wt% samples showed only minor corrosion, whereas the 1.5 wt% sample accumulated a substantial amount of corrosion products on its surface; 3) SKPFM tests demonstrated that the 0 wt% sample had the minimum surface ﬂuctuation range was 20–90 nm, reﬂecting minimal surface height variation, which correlates with lower corrosion levels. Additionally, the surface potential ﬂuctuation range was 30–10 mV, indicating a low amplitude of potential ﬂuctuation. The reduced surface potential in higher La content alloys resulted in thinner Cr2O3 passive ﬁlms, decreasing their resistance to Cl penetration and thereby reducing corrosion resistance. Surface roughness tests further conﬁrmed that the 0 wt% sample exhibited minimal surface ﬂuctuation and shallower microporous corrosion depths, correlating with its superior corrosion resistance.

### 10.3390/ma14010123
page1_block2 Article Investigation of the Erosion Damage Mechanism and Erosion Prediction of Boronized Coatings at Elevated Temperatures
page1_block9 Coatings at Elevated Temperatures.
page1_block27 1 School of Chemical Engineering and Technology, Xi’an Jiaotong University, Xi’an 710049, China; liuxicai@xjtu.edu.cn (L.-X.C.); yunli@mail.xjtu.edu.cn (Y.L.); heyoox163@163.com (Y.H.); a772601806@stu.xjtu.edu.cn (Z.-K.L.) 2 State Key Laboratory of Multiphase Flow in Power Engineering, Xi’an Jiaotong University, Xi’an 710049, China; leyoushijie@163.com * Correspondence: sswang@mail.xjtu.edu.cn; Tel.: +86-29-82667808
page1_block28 Abstract: In this study, the high temperature erosion mechanisms and damage characteristics of a boronized coating have been systematically studied by employing an improved high-temperature ac- celerated erosion test bench and impact contact theory analyses. Within the scope of the experimental parameters, the erosion rate of the boronized coating under the same erosion conditions was observed to be only one half to one-twelfth of the erosion rate of the substrate. Furthermore, the boronized coating was noted to be less sensitive to the speed of the erosion particles than the plastic substrate, thus, indicating superior and more stable erosion resistance than the base material. The boronized coating exhibited typical brittle fracture characteristics under impact by the high-speed particles. When the particle impact normal stress exceeded the critical stress for crack propagation owing to the coating defects, the surface and subsurface layers of the coating initially formed horizontal and vertical micro-cracks, followed by their gradual expansion and intersection. After destabilization, the brittle coating material was peeled layer-by-layer from the surface of the test piece. At the same incident speed, as the particle size was increased from 65 µm to 226 µm and 336 µm, the size (width) of the erosion cracks on the coating surface increased from 1 µm to 30 µm and 100 µm respectively. Correspondingly, the erosion damage thickness of the coating was enhanced from 15 µm to 50 µm and 100 µm. In the case of the quartz sand particle size exceeding 300 µm, the dual-phase boronized coating did not provide effective protection to the substrate. Furthermore, based on the elastoplastic fracture theory, a prediction model for the erosion weight loss of the boronized coatings within the effective thickness range has been proposed in this study.
page1_block29 Keywords: boronized coating; erosion damage mechanism; high-temperature erosion experiment; particle size; erosion prediction
page2_block3 At present, the commonly used method to overcome the particle erosion issue is to coat the surface of the material with a hard coating so as to improve its passive anti-wear performance. Two main types of strengthening coatings are commonly used. In the ﬁrst case, interstitial solid solutions are formed on a metal surface through atomic diffusion, such as the thermal spray coating [7]. In other case, new compounds are formed on the surface through the reaction of the diffused atoms and base metal, such as boronized coatings [8]. Compared with the thermal spray coating, the thermal diffusion boronized coating exhibits compact structure and higher coating hardness, along with superior erosion resistance [9,10]. Therefore, the boronized coating is widely used in the industrial processes for strengthening of the substrates.
page2_block4 With respect to the anti-wear performance of the boronized coating, Biddulph et al. [11] summarized that the boronized coating exhibited excellent resistance to sliding and erosion wear, and extended the life of the mold by more than 10 times. Based on the disc wear test, Tabur et al. [12] pointed out that, compared with the base material AISI8620 steel, the boronized coating enhanced the wear resistance of the material by ﬁve times. Saduman et al. [13] conducted a low-speed (1 m/s) sliding wear test using a similar test bench and observed that the wear rate of the boronized coating was only 10% of that of the AISI4140 substrate. The dry sliding wear test conducted by Taktak [14] revealed that, at 600 °C, the wear resistance of the boronized coating was 3 and 2.5 times that of the base materials 52100 steel and 440C steel, respectively. Medvedovski et al. [15] simulated the wear behavior of the oil mining equipment components using the reciprocating sliding wear test bench. It was observed that, due to the high hardness and bonding strength of the dual-phase boronized coating, its wear-corrosion resistance was 30 times that of the carbon steel and other coatings (e.g., electroplated nickel coatings). In fact, the boronized coating was signiﬁcantly effective for the low alloy steels, chromium-molybdenum-based alloy steels and cobalt-based alloys, improving the wear resistance of the substrates by four to ﬁve times [16].
page2_block5 To study the anti-erosion performance of the boronized coating, Qureshi et al. [17] used 75 µm chromite particles as the erosion particles with impact velocities of 228~304 m/s and impact angles of 22.5◦and 90◦. The erosion rate of the boronized coating after process optimization was only one-third of that of the substrate 403SS. B.S. Mann [18] employed a rotating disc to model the erosion environment of the steam turbine blades at room temperature. It was reported that the erosion resistance of the boronized coating was 7.5 to 9.2 times that of the blade substrate (12Cr steel). By employing systematic high-temperature erosion tests, Wang et al. [19] concluded that the collapse erosion occurred in a 10 µm-thick boronized coating when the erosion particles were at diverse attacking angles. On the other hand, the erosion took place in a 20 µm-thick boronized coating when the erosion particles were at 75◦and 90◦attack angles. The coating was noted to swiftly fall off locally and fail. In another study, Cai et al. [20] conducted high-temperature erosion tests on three typical boronized coatings and observed that the single-phase Fe2B coatings exhibited a superior erosion resistance to that of the dual-phase boronized coatings. The numerical simulation results [21] further conﬁrmed the experimental ﬁndings. Prescott [22] used 150 µm quartz sand particles to impact different boronized coatings and substrate test pieces at a speed of 36 m/s by employing the sandblasting test setup. It was reported that the erosion rate of the dual-phase boronized coating at a large erosion angle was equivalent to that of 316 stainless steel and ordinary carbon steel, however the erosion rate at a small angle was noted to be much smaller.
page3_block2 the high temperature erosion test data and numerical simulation results, the erosion rate prediction model for the boronized coatings under high temperature service environments has not been established. Such a model is vitally needed for the service life prediction and maintenance cycle formulation of boronized coatings. In this study, a systematic experi- mental investigation of the high-temperature particle erosion of the typical duplex phase boronized coatings was carried out by using an improved high-temperature accelerated erosion test bench. Combined with the microscopic and impact contact theory analyses, the erosion failure mechanism and inﬂuence of the erosion particle size on the erosion damage characteristics of the boronized coatings were revealed. Furthermore, based on the elastoplastic indentation and rupture theory, by analyzing and ﬁtting the experimental data, a prediction model for the erosion rate of the boronized coatings was established. The ﬁndings from this study provide vital insights about the high temperature erosion damage mechanism of the brittle coatings, rational selection of the boronized coatings for different industrial applications and prediction of the coating service life.
page3_block4 Figure 1a is the physical diagram of the high-temperature accelerated erosion test system, whereas Figure 1b presents the three-dimensional model of the erosion test section. The working principle of the experimental system is as follows: gas from the combustion chamber is divided into two streams, one directly enters the test chamber to heat the specimen and establish a high-temperature erosion environment, whereas the other stream enters the acceleration nozzle to accelerate the solid particles. The solid particles from the screw feeder are directly transported by the high-pressure airﬂow to the throat of the accel- eration nozzle. After being accelerated by the high-temperature gas, a uniform two-phase gas–solid mixture meeting the test requirements of temperature and speed is obtained. Subsequently, the solid particles hit the target material installed in the cavity of the test section at a certain inclination (β) to produce the corresponding erosion. The diameter of the main gas pipeline of the experimental system was 80 mm, the outlet diameter of the acceleration nozzle was 9 mm, and the diameter of the particle-conveying pipe at the outlet of the feeder was 4 mm.
page3_block7 Figure 1. High-temperature accelerated erosion test system: (a) Image of the test system (b) 3D model of the test section.
page4_block2 In order to avoid the inﬂuence of the concentrated erosion by the particles in the accelerating nozzle on the measurement of the material erosion rate (as observed in the literature studies), an air ﬂow pre-swirl device was installed before the accelerating nozzle, as shown in Figure 1. By adjusting the angle of the bafﬂe, the main airﬂow produced a moderate swirl before entering the accelerating nozzle, as shown in Figure 2a. As the gas enters the accelerating nozzle and air velocity increases, the vortex dissipation is increased, with the vortex intensity increased correspondingly until the vortex intensity reaches the extreme value as the airﬂow reaches the nozzle throat, as shown in Figure 2b. Subsequently, the airﬂow enters the straight pipe section, and the factors that promote an enhancement in the vortex intensity are signiﬁcantly weakened. On the other hand, the airﬂow velocity and vortex dissipation are still maintained at a high level, thus, the swirling intensity becomes weak, leading to the formation of a weak concentric swirl, as shown in Figure 2c. As the solid particles accelerate along the ﬂow direction in the accelerating nozzle, the rotating airﬂow drags them to produce the radial diffusion. The rotating kinetic energy of the airﬂow is gradually dissipated on interaction with the wall and solid particles, and the spatial distribution of the particles becomes stable as the swirling ﬂow dissipates. Finally, a uniform high-speed two-phase gas–solid jet is formed. Figure 3 shows the surface erosion morphology of the test piece before and after the improvement of the test section. It can be observed that the improved erosion test section was effective in avoiding the concentrated erosion (local pits) by the particles on the target surface, thus, improving the test accuracy.
page4_block27 In the high temperature erosion test, in order to verify the repeatability of the ex- perimental ﬁndings under the same testing conditions, the experiments were performed on four targets in the same material by the rapid switching of the rotating mechanism.
page5_block2 The erosion rate of the target can be obtained by measuring the cumulative mass mp of the particles involved in the erosion, as well as the mass m1 and m2 of the target before and after the erosion phenomenon. During the test, mp was measured by using an electronic balance, while m1 and m2 were determined by employing BS224S precision electronic balance from Sartorius, Germany, with a measurement accuracy of 0.1 mg.
page5_block3 Particle image velocimetry (PIV) was used to measure the average velocity of the particles hitting the target surface, and the test principle has been presented in Figure 4a. The sheet light emitted by the Yttrium Aluminum Garnet (YAG) dual-pulse laser illumi- nates the ﬂow ﬁeld test area. In the direction perpendicular to the sheet light surface, an ImagePro4M CCD camera made from LaVision inc. of Germany is used to capture the moving images of the particles in the area illuminated by the sheet light. In the study, the YAG dual-pulse laser had a wavelength of 532 nm, and each pulse energy was 200 mJ. Furthermore, the pulse width and pulse frequency values were 5 ns and 15 Hz, respec- tively. The laser dual-pulse interval was adjusted from 0.5 µs to 0.1 ms, thus, realizing the measurement of the ﬂow ﬁeld from low to high speed. The ImagePro4M CCD image sensor had a pixel size of 2048 × 2048, with a sensing area of 15.1 mm × 15.1 mm and a maximum sampling rate of 16 fps. Using DiVis 8.0 software to perform the cross-correlation processing of the graphics, the particle velocity ﬁeld was ﬁnally obtained, as shown in Figure 4b.
page5_block6 A VEGAII XUM scanning electron microscope (SEM) made from TESCAN inc. of Czech Republic was used to analyze the surface erosion morphology and coating thickness of the test piece. The average thickness of the coating was determined by taking the average of the thickness at 15 different locations. The composition of the boronized coating was conﬁrmed by using SEM in the backscattering mode (BSE) and XRD analysis. A Buehler MICROMET 5104 automatic microhardness tester was used to measure the hardness of the polished cross-section of the boronized coating, at an applied load of 1 N, as shown in Figure 5. In addition, the Vickers hardness indentation method was used to measure the fracture toughness of the boronized coating, at an applied load of 3 N. The speciﬁc calculation procedure for the determination of the fracture toughness can be found in the literature [20]. In order to ascertain the reproducibility of the test data, the coating hardness and fracture toughness were reported after averaging 10 measurements under the same conditions.
page6_block5 The base material of the test target was martensitic stainless steel 2Cr10MoVNbN. Its yield and tensile strength at room temperature were 860 MPa and 980 MPa, respectively. The elongation and reduction in area were 16% and 56%, respectively, with a hardness of 320 HB. The boronized coating was obtained by applying the solid boronizing powder to the stainless-steel substrate at a temperature of 1123~1273 K [20]. The detailed process is as follows: the substrate test piece was kept in the boronizing furnace for 10 h at a temperature of 1273 K, followed by cooling in air to room temperature. Finally, the tempering was carried out at 943 K for 8 h. As shown in Figure 6, in the backscattering mode, the prepared boronized coating exhibited a compact and typical dual-phase structure, with the top FeB phase and bottom Fe2B phase accounting for about 50% each and exhibiting the characteristics of the tooth-like growth. The microscopic analysis revealed that the total thickness of the boronized coating was 120 µm. Furthermore, the hardness and porosity of the coating were measured to be 1800 HV and <0.5%, respectively.
page6_block7 Figure 6. Cross-sectional morphology in backscattering (BSE) mode.
page6_block8 In the experiment, quartz sand particles were used as the erosion particles. The self- developed particle morphology and size analysis software was employed to analyze the size distribution of the erosion particles. The micro-morphology and particle size distribution of the quartz sand particles of different sizes are presented in Figures 7 and 8. The quartz sand particles are noted to be angular, with the Sauter average diameters of the three kinds of particles as 65 µm, 226 µm and 336 µm.
page7_block3 Figure 7. Microscopic morphology of the sand particles used in the test: (a) D32 = 65 µm; (b) D32 = 226 µm; (c) D32 = 336 µm.
page7_block6 Through systematic numerical simulation, the speed of the solid particles impacting the turbine blades was observed to be in the range 200 m/s~350 m/s [23], thus, two particle impact speeds of 210 m/s and 320 m/s were chosen. Based on the intake temperature of the supercritical steam turbine and ﬁnal stage compression temperature of the aero-engine compressor, 566 ◦C was selected as the test temperature. For each sample, the coating erosion tests were conducted at seven particle incidence angles of 18◦, 24◦, 30◦, 36◦, 45◦, 60◦, 75◦and 90◦, keeping the same particle impact speed and air temperature.
page8_block2 sensitive to the particle speed than the plastic target, thus, exhibiting higher and stable erosion resistance than the base material.
page8_block5 Figure 10 shows the surface erosion morphology of the substrate and boronized coat- ing after the erosion by the 226 µm quartz sand particles. As can be seen from Figure 10a, the surface of the substrate shows obvious traces of micro-cutting and furrowing. During the micro-cutting by the particle edges and corners, most of the traces on the plastic ma- terial were removed at once to form the wear debris. During furrowing and lip forming, the deformed lips on both sides and ends of the furrow were repeatedly deformed and fatigued owing to the multiple impacts, followed by falling off to form the wear debris. As can be seen from Figure 10b,c due to the superior hardness of the boronized coating, the erosion area did not exhibit large-scale plastic deformation. However, when the impact normal stress exceeded certain critical stress, the microcracks generated at the coating defects began to multiply and expand, thus, leading to the formation of the horizontal and longitudinal microcracks on the surface and subsurface of the coating. The cracks gradually expanded and intersected, and the surface of the specimen was peeled off layer-by-layer due to destabilization.
page8_block9 Figure 10. Erosion morphology of the target surface at 210 m/s, 566 °C and 30◦condition (D32 = 226 µm): (a) Surface morphology of the substrate (b) Surface morphology of the boronized coating; (c) Cross-section morphology of the boronized coating.
page9_block2 The comprehensive high-temperature erosion (Figure 9) and micro-erosion morphol- ogy (Figure 10) analyses reveal that the micro-cutting erosion rate of the quartz sand particles on the substrate at a small impact angle was much higher than that of the layer- by-layer peeling observed after crack propagation and coating destabilization. Therefore, for industrial applications where erosion happens by the particles at small impact angles, incorporation of the boronized coatings can effectively enhance the erosion resistance of the materials. On the other hand, at a large impact angle, due to the continuous enhancement of the particle impact normal stress at the same speed, the peeling rate of the unstable brittle coating materials due to the propagation of cracks in the coating increases, thus, the erosion rate of the boronized coating gradually increases. The observed variation of the erosion rate of the boronized coating as a function of the erosion angle is consistent with the literature reports [9,18,19,24]. The plastic substrate exhibited a lower rate of plastic deformation erosion under the impact of the particles at a large incident angle than that of micro-cutting and furrow erosion at a small angle, thus, the erosion rate gradually decreased as the incident angle increased beyond 30◦.
page9_block6 For the dual-phase boronized coating investigated in this study, the results from the Vickers hardness analysis in reference [20] show that the fracture toughness of the FeB coating on the top is 1.5 MPa·m1/2, whereas the fracture toughness of the Fe2B coating on the bottom is 5.42 MPa·m1/2. From Equation (1), the critical stress value of the internal crack propagation for the FeB coating is calculated to be in the range 0.85 GPa~2.68 GPa, whereas the critical stress value of the internal crack propagation for the Fe2B coating is determined to be in the range 3.06 GPa~9.67 GPa.
page9_block7 The actual orientation of the angular quartz sand particles in contact with the wall surface of the coated target is difﬁcult to be determined, thus, an equivalent spherical quartz sand particle of D32 = 226 µm impacting on the dual-phase boronized coating was taken as the research object in this study, and the transient impact stress of the spherical particle impacting normal to the coating surface was theoretically calculated.
page9_block8 According to the Hertz’s contact theory, the contact force F during the normal impact of a hard spherical particle on the surface of an inﬁnitely large target can be expressed as [27]:
